--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is MINIMISED (lower = better)
- Items from one half decrease score by 1 (GOOD), items from the other half increase by 1 (BAD)
- We need robots to consistently collect from the GOOD half (one color) and deliver to the nest (WHITE floor)
- The nest is on the WHITE side, source is on the BLACK side
- BLUE walls vs GREEN walls distinguish the two halves

**Analysis of results:**
- Champion: simple exploration (score -0.16) - robots randomly find and deliver items
- The key insight: robots need to go to BLACK (source), pick up items, return to WHITE (nest)
- We should try to navigate toward one color (BLUE or GREEN) and away from the other
- The RED wall marks the nest side - robots could navigate toward RED to return

**Strategy:**
- GoToColor toward BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) to find the good source half
- GoToColor toward RED (clr=1) to return to nest
- Use floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest) to trigger transitions
- Robots carrying items from the decreasing-score half should go to WHITE (nest)

**Key colors:** 1=red (nest wall), 2=green (one source half), 3=blue (other source half), 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Plan:**
1. Keep champion (survival)
2. Exploit champion with minor variants (different rwm values)
3. Exploit 2-state machines: explore → go to source, then return to nest
4. Try directed foraging: GoToColor(BLUE) on black floor → GoToColor(RED) on white floor
5. Try GoAwayColor(GREEN) to prefer BLUE side, then navigate back

Let me design machines that systematically go to BLUE side (score decreasing) and return via RED wall:

- State 0: GoToColor RED (clr=1) - go to nest wall to deliver
- State 1: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) - go to blue source to pick up good items
- Transition: on BLACK floor (at source) → go back; on WHITE floor (at nest) → go to source

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**
The champion uses:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) → transition on BLACK floor (p=0.8) back to state 0 (self-loop isn't allowed... wait, it goes to destination 0 which with 2 states leaving state 0 means "the other state" = state 1? Let me re-read)

Wait, re-reading: `--n0x0 0` means destination index 0 among the OTHER states. With 2 states (0 and 1), leaving state 0, the only other state is state 1, so D=0 targets state 1. So:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), on BLACK floor → go to state 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (clr=1), on WHITE floor → go to state 0

This is perfect: go to BLUE source (black floor), pick up item, then go to RED/nest wall (white floor), deliver, repeat. BLUE items must be the good ones (decrease score).

The second best uses GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2) which also steers toward BLUE side effectively.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion: vary probabilities (0.7-0.95 range for BLACK trigger, 0.8-0.95 for WHITE trigger)
3. Try adding a gray-floor transition or extra state
4. Try GoToColor BLUE with additional transitions for robustness
5. Try 3-state variants with exploration buffer

Note: LED colors must be {0, 4, 5, 6} only!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis:**
The champion structure is very clear:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), transition on BLACK floor (p=0.7) → go to state 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (clr=1), transition on WHITE floor (p=0.85) → go to state 0

This works perfectly: robots go to the BLUE source (black floor), pick up good items, then navigate to the RED wall (nest, white floor) to deliver them.

The key parameters to tune are the two probabilities: p for BLACK trigger (currently 0.7) and p for WHITE trigger (currently 0.85).

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Fine-tune probabilities around 0.7 and 0.85
- Try slightly lower BLACK trigger (0.6, 0.65) to spend more time at source
- Try slightly higher WHITE trigger (0.9, 0.95) to more reliably deliver
- Add a second transition as a safety (e.g., stochastic fallback)

**Exploration strategy:**
- Try 3-state variants with an exploration buffer state
- Try using GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2) instead of GoToColor BLUE
- Try swapping which color is targeted (maybe GREEN is the good half)
- Try adding neighbor-based transitions

Note: LED colors MUST be {0, 4, 5, 6} only!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) with 2 transitions (BLACK p=0.7, WHITE p=0.85 both → state 1), then GoToColor RED (clr=1) with WHITE trigger (p=0.85) back to state 0
- The second transition in state 0 (on WHITE floor, p=0.85 → state 1) is interesting: when already at nest, also go to return state. This creates a faster loop.
- GoToColor GREEN (clr=2) is BAD (score +12.93) - confirms BLUE is the good source
- Adding stochastic transitions (C=5) to state 1 was very bad - avoid

**Parameter tuning insights:**
- BLACK trigger in state 0: 0.7 seems optimal (0.6-0.75 range all competitive)
- WHITE trigger in state 1: 0.85 is consistent across all top performers
- The champion has TWO transitions in state 0: BLACK (p=0.7) AND WHITE (p=0.85)

**Exploitation plan:**
- Vary the two probabilities in the champion structure
- Try adding WHITE trigger back to state 0 with different values
- Try adding a stochastic or gray-floor secondary trigger in state 1

**Exploration plan:**
- Try 3-state with exploration state between source and nest
- Try using GoAwayColor RED (clr=1, B=9) to escape nest after delivery
- Try different combinations of triggers

## Reasoning

**Clear pattern emerging:**
The champion structure is locked in:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), transitions: BLACK floor (p=0.75) → state 1, WHITE floor (p=0.85) → state 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (clr=1), transition: WHITE floor (p=0.85) → state 0

The second transition in state 0 (WHITE floor → state 1) makes sense: if a robot is already at the nest (white floor) in the GoToBlue state, it should switch to GoToRed to get back... wait, that seems backwards. Actually state 1 IS GoToRed (going to nest). So if we're in state 0 (GoToBlue) and we're on WHITE floor, we go to state 1 (GoToRed)? That would mean going away from nest... 

Actually re-reading: transitions are probabilistic. On WHITE floor while in GoToBlue state, switching to GoToRed (p=0.85) means robots that accidentally reach the nest while heading to source will correctly switch to delivery mode. This creates robustness.

**Parameter space to explore:**
- p(BLACK) in state 0: 0.7-0.8 range (0.75 is best)
- p(WHITE) in state 0: 0.8-0.95 range (0.85 is best)
- p(WHITE) in state 1: 0.8-0.95 range (0.85 is best)

**Key findings:**
- Adding gray-floor transition (C=1) to state 0 was bad (-1.46)
- 3-state and 4-state variants are worse
- The 2-state, 2-transition structure with exact parameters is very strong

Let me focus on fine-tuning around the champion and a few careful explorations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation assessment:**
The champion hasn't improved since round 5 (-14.78). The 2-state structure with GoToColor BLUE → GoToColor RED is clearly the right topology. All top performers this round are variants of the same structure.

**Key observations:**
- Champion: p(BLACK)=0.75, p(WHITE in s0)=0.85, p(WHITE in s1)=0.85
- Adding a small BLACK floor trigger to state 1 (p=0.08) gave -14.17 (close but not better)
- p(WHITE in s0)=0.9 was WORSE (-12.55) - avoid higher values there
- GoAwayColor GREEN instead of GoToRed was bad
- 3-state variants are consistently worse

**Fine-tuning focus:**
The champion is at a local optimum. Let me try:
1. Slightly different p values around 0.75/0.85
2. Adding a BLACK floor trigger to state 1 at very low probability (like 0.08 worked well)
3. Try p(WHITE in s0) slightly lower (0.82, 0.83)
4. Try adding a stochastic trigger (C=5) at very low probability to state 1 to encourage state switching
5. Explore if a 3-transition state 0 with BLACK + WHITE + something else helps

**LED colors:** only {0, 4, 5, 6}!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation:** The champion is stuck at -14.78 for 3 rounds. The 2-state structure GoToBlue/GoToRed is clearly optimal topology. Fine-tuning isn't breaking through.

**Key lessons:**
- Adding 3rd transition with C=5 (stochastic) to state 0 was bad (-3.49)
- Adding C=7 (color perception) to state 1 was very bad (+10.80, +10.90)
- Adding small BLACK trigger to state 1 (p=0.07-0.1) gives ~-13 to -14 range
- The champion's exact parameters seem near-optimal for this topology

**Why isn't it improving?** The score variance is high (stochastic arena). The champion at -14.78 might actually be close to the true optimum for this topology.

**Strategy for round 8:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Run multiple copies with slight parameter variations to reduce variance effects
3. Try very small perturbations: p values ±0.02-0.03
4. Try a completely different approach: 4-state with explicit "loaded/unloaded" tracking using LED colors
5. Try adding a neighbor-based condition to encourage spreading

**Avoid:** Color perception (C=7) in state 1, stochastic (C=5) in state 0, 3-state variants

**New exploration idea:** What if we try GoToColor BLUE with a very high probability trigger (close to 1.0) so robots almost always switch immediately on black floor? Or very low (0.5) to sample more?

## Reasoning

**Progress:** New champion at -15.35! The key addition was a small BLACK floor trigger (p=0.07) in state 1. This makes sense: if robots are heading to the nest (GoToRed) and hit the black floor, they switch back to GoToBlue - catching any robots that overshot into the source area.

**Champion structure:**
- State 0: GoToBlue, on BLACK→s1 (p=0.75), on WHITE→s1 (p=0.85)
- State 1: GoToRed, on WHITE→s0 (p=0.85), on BLACK→s0 (p=0.07)

**What to tune:**
- The BLACK trigger in state 1 (0.07) - try 0.05, 0.06, 0.08, 0.09, 0.10
- The WHITE trigger in state 0 (0.85) - try 0.83, 0.84, 0.86
- The BLACK trigger in state 0 (0.75) - try 0.73, 0.74, 0.76, 0.77
- The WHITE trigger in state 1 (0.85) - try 0.84, 0.86

**Avoid:**
- C=1 (gray floor) transitions - bad
- C=7 (color perception) - very bad
- p=0.88 in state 1 WHITE trigger - bad
- p=0.06 for BLACK trigger in state 1 with p=0.88 WHITE - bad

**Exploration:**
- Try adding BLACK trigger to state 0 as well (already there at 0.75) and WHITE to state 1 (already at 0.85) - the champion has both, so try a 3-transition state 1
- Try swapping: add a stochastic very-low probability trigger

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 25 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.46 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
